Text:"Any Time, Any Space: World-State-Centric Framework for Agentic Video Generation Weijie Wang1, Haomin Zhang1, Ye Li1, Akide Liu2 Yefei He1 Donny Y. Chen2 1 Zhejiang University Bohan Zhuang1, 3 BAIR, UC Berkeley Zeyu Zhang3 Haoyu Zhao1 4 Adelaide University 2 Monash University Feng Chen Abstract. Long-form agentic video generation must turn screenplay into shots that remain spatially and temporally consistent across nonconsecutive requests. Passing prompts or recent frames between shots does not provide shared spatial frame for revisiting scene or time-indexed record for replaying an earlier event. We present Any Time, Any Space (ATAS), which compiles screenplay into shot requests with explicit location, camera, and event-time references. Before generation, it retrieves relevant evidence from scene memory of registered static geometry and an event memory of dynamic observations indexed by scene, event, and time. After generation, ATAS reconstructs and registers the clip. It updates memory only when review and state checks accept the observation, while film delivery remains independent of memory admission. Across four video generators, ATAS improves all reported cross-shot consistency measures and achieves the highest aggregate benchmark score for each backbone. It also outperforms the compared agents and 3D-guided methods on target-image spatial revisit, leads three event-recapture criteria, and receives the highest human ratings on all six criteria. Date: October 13, 2026 Project Page: http://lhmd.top/atas Keywords: Long Video Generation, Agentic Framework, Multi-shot Video Generation Correspondence: bohan.zhuang@zju.edu.cn Code: https://github.com/ziplab/ATAS Agentic video generation translates screenplay into sequence of video shots by coordinating shot planning, reference selection, and video synthesis. As story unfolds across locations, times, and viewpoints, its coherence depends on preserving the spatial and temporal relationships established in earlier shots. "

Text:"More Methods, Better Agents? Reality Check on Self-Distillation in Agentic Reinforcement Learning Linhao Zhong1,2,*, Bozhen Fang1,*, Xuehao Wang1, Duochao Shi1, Zekai Luo1, Guangming Yao2, Yi Yuan2, Hao Chen1, Jingdong Chen2, Chunhua Shen1,2 1Zhejiang University, State Lab of CAD & CG 2Ant Group *Equal contribution. Self-distillation in agentic reinforcement learning has produced growing number of methods. However, existing evaluations often reuse published baseline scores under differing experimental conditions, provide few direct comparisons among contemporaneous methods, and give limited attention to training stability and computational cost. These limitations obscure the source of reported gains and the practical strengths and weaknesses of competing methods. Costly agent training further motivates common basis for comparison. To address these gaps, we compare ten representative methods against GRPO on ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, Search-QA, and WebShop across three model sizes. Our study comprises 132 training runs totaling 12,124.4 recorded H800 GPU-hours, with repeated evaluations over eight rollout seeds for the pretrained backbones and all eleven trained methods. Beyond final task performance, we examine training progress, stability, and measured computational cost. Our evaluation yields three key findings. First, gains over GRPO remain limited in scope, with no method consistently outperforming it across settings. Second, strong final performance can mask unstable training. Third, additional training cost does not consistently translate into better performance. We establish this benchmark to support fair, comprehensive comparison and guide future research toward reliable and cost-effective improvements in agent training. Date: October 2026 Project page: https://aim-uofa.github.io/Self-Distillation-AgentRL GitHub repo: https://github.com/aim-uofa/Self-Distillation-AgentRL Large language model (LLM) agents interact with environments through sequences of actions a"

Text:"2026-10-15 LatentLoop: Scaling Looped MoE Models with Asymmetric Compute Allocation Zefeng He1,2,*, Yunkai Chai1,2,*, Xiaoyang Chen2,3,*, Tong Zhu2,*, Zongliang Wu2, Yun Luo2, Yafu Li2,4, and Yu Cheng5 1SJTU, 2Shanghai AI Lab, 3USTC, 4CUHK, 5NTU Core contributors, Corresponding author Project Page Code Abstract Looped Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models combine parameter reuse with sparse computation to improve performance under matched budgets. Prior work demonstrates that different loop visits can play different roles even when they share parameters. To probe the contributions of different loop visits, we learn separate residual coefficients in model that applies shared block twice, observing smaller scale in the first visit. Fixed coefficient experiments show that reducing the first visits scale preserves or improves performance, whereas the same reductions to the second hurt it. This asymmetry motivates us to explore asymmetric compute allocation. We propose LatentLoop, which compresses the first visits sequence, retains full resolution in the second, and reallocates saved computation to increase the hidden size. We further learn how strongly the first visit updates the full sequence, and share MoE weights across visits while using separate attention parameters to accommodate their different sequence resolutions. Across 15 configurations spanning 1.3B7B parameters, with the largest budget exceeding 1021 FLOPs, LatentLoop consistently lowers validation NLL. At fitted NLL of 1.988, predicted compute savings are 19.0% and 7.9% relative to vanilla MoE and the looped baseline. It also improves average scores on nine benchmarks at all sizes in 0-shot and 5-shot settings. These results suggest asymmetric compute allocation is promising direction for looped MoE models. 1. Introduction Looped Transformers (Bai et al., 2019; Dehghani et al., 2018; Geiping et al., 2026; Prairie et al., 2026; Zhu et al., 2025) increase effective depth by repeatedly applying shared layers, offer"

Text:"KV4-RL: Unlocking Faster Asynchronous Rollouts for RLVR with INT4 KV Cache Quantization Sihwan Park1, Sung-Yub Kim2, Doohyuk Jang1 and Eunho Yang1,3, 1KAIST, 2NAVER Cloud, 3AITRICS, Corresponding Author Abstract. Asynchronous rollout improves the efficiency of reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) by reducing under-utilization during long-tail generation. Sustained concurrency also concentrates context processing into fewer decode steps, increasing the KV workload per step. We characterize this KV concentration and find that KV cache quantization delivers substantially greater acceleration than weight-only and weight-activation quantization in our matched-workload comparisons. However, INT4 KV rollout can degrade learning even with token-level truncated importance sampling (TIS). We propose KV4-RL, which combines calibration-free INT4 KV quantization with selective INT8 protection of attention sinks and recent context. Protecting bounded number of positions reduces sampler mismatch while retaining most of the speedup from INT4. Across 400 iterations of Qwen3-8B-Base GRPO training, KV4-RL achieves 1.80 cumulative rollout speedup and 1.27 cumulative full-iteration speedup over BF16 rollout, reaching 1.97 rollout speedup over the final 50 iterations. Under common BF16 evaluator, its final six-benchmark mean accuracy is within 0.5 percentage points of BF16-rollout training for both Qwen3-4B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base. 1. Modern LLM agents still reason and interact through text, while latent reasoning offers an efficient internal medium but lacks native tool access and reasoning transparency. We bridge these two directions with Hybrid Reasoning Agents (HybridAgents), which dynamically navigate between latent reasoning and text-based interaction throughout unified generation process. Architecturally, lightweight HybridLink enables seamless transitions between text and latent modes, while the agents continuous working memory preserves lossless hybrid context across multiple turns. These designs preserve tool-integrated agentic capabilities while making latent thoughts directly inspectable. Building on this architecture, we further develop dedicated post-training algorithms for hybrid reasoning: HybridSFT stabilizes tool-integrated hybrid generation by teaching the backbone to coordinate with HybridLink, then HybridRL scales reasoning by learning when to think in latent space and when to act through text. We evaluate HybridAgent at 3 model scales across 11 benchmarks covering mathematics, science, medicine, search, and agentic reasoning. Compared with frontier LLM Agents and agentic frameworks spanning both text-based and latent methods, HybridAgent consistently improves performance by 10.4%, while delivering 35.5%54.7% token reduction, 1.82.6 faster inference, and 2.73.7 cost savings. Code: https://github.com/togethercomputer/HybridAgent Correspondence: jiaru@stanford.edu, jamesz@stanford.edu Figure 1 HybridAgent Architecture Overview. Text:"Wenxuan Zhu1 Shuming Liu1 Bang An1 Aznaur Aliev1 Bing Li1 Yibo Yang2 Bernard Ghanem1 1King Abdullah University of Science and Technology 2Shanghai Jiao Tong University Abstract Hybrid reasoning models such as the Qwen3 series switch between think mode and no-think mode through few chat-template tokens. popular recipe trains such models with reinforcement learning in no-think mode and reports improved reasoning performance. We provide evidence that much of this gain can be explained by thinking leakage: the gain comes largely from partially reactivating the reasoning abilities of the original models think mode instead of learning enhanced reasoning behavior. We train Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B with GRPO in no-think mode. As accuracy improves, responses become longer and more reflective, are increasingly judged by the original model to resemble its own thinking, and the models internal activations shift toward its think mode. We then investigate at the level of weights, activations, and attention heads: scaling the RL update further elicits think-mode behavior and approaches think-mode accuracy but does not exceed it; in Qwen3-8B, replacing low-dimensional think-related activation subspace of the RL-trained model with its original-model value removes most of the RL gain, and the reverse exchange transfers considerable fraction of the gain to the original model; and the RL-induced shift passes through the attention heads that read the empty think block. We further identify possible mechanism in the GRPO training signal: responses favored by the advantage-weighted update are more think-like, and gradient step computed from this signal moves the model toward its think state. Thus, gains in no-think mode can largely reflect reactivation of reasoning abilities already present in the original model. Code is available on GitHub. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is widely used to improve the reasoning of large language models [6, 17]. rule-based verifier chec"

Text:"APT: FEEDBACK-DRIVEN AGENTIC POST-TRAINING FOR TEXT-TO-IMAGE GENERATION Sirui Zhang1,2 Fangyun Wei2 Dong Chen2 Yunuo Chen5 Baining Guo2 Zheng-Jun Zha1 1 University of Science and Technology of China 3 University of Sydney {zsr200901, zhazj}@mail.ustc.edu.cn {fawe, doch, bainguo}@microsoft.com 4 Fudan University Jinjing Zhao3 Yitong Wang4 2 Microsoft Research Asia 5 Shanghai Jiao Tong University Figure 1: APT (Agentic Post-Training) turns post-training into fully automated, evaluationdriven adaptive loop. Given user-specified evaluation scope, the Evaluation Set Generation Agent constructs diverse evaluation set consisting of promptevaluation rubric pairs; the Diagnosis Agent evaluates the current model on these pairs to identify its strengths and failure modes; and the RL Data Generation Agent adaptively samples next-round training prompts, prioritizing underperforming capabilities. The updated model is then re-evaluated in the next round, allowing the training distribution to continuously adapt to the models evolving capability profile. This design eliminates the need for manually curated post-training data and makes APT model-agnostic, benchmark-agnostic, architecture-agnostic, and fully automated. "

Ideally, AI-generated plans could be carried out directly by robotic systems, but reliable automation remains difficult in complex, open-world laboratory environments, where experiments often involve fine-grained manipulation, long-horizon procedures, and substantial variation across tasks and setups. Humans therefore remain an important execution interface between AI-generated plans and physical experiments, creating need for clear and effective human-facing experimental guidance. Because laboratory procedures are inherently visual, spatial, and dynamic, video is particularly well suited to communicating apparatus configurations, manipulation actions, temporal dependencies, and state changes. Recent advances in video generation now make it possible to synthesize experimental demonstrations directly from an initial workspace image and natural-language instruction. We introduce LabInstruct, benchmark for situated instructional video generation in real laboratories. LabInstruct contains 204 tasks across 5 scientific disciplines, with real reference executions and structured annotations of objects, actions, contacts, and state transitions. However, practical mechanisms for skill ownership verification and leakage tracing remain underdeveloped. Watermark embedding before distribution offers potential solution, but structured, compact skill instructions impose stringent requirements for factual and procedural accuracy. To address these challenges, we introduce SkillMarker, KGW-based watermarking method that jointly targets functionality preservation and strong watermark embedding. SkillMarker combines quality-constrained rewriting with signaloriented candidate search to strengthen watermark signals while preserving essential instructions. We develop toolkit for watermark embedding, quality auditing, and detection. Across four datasets, SkillMarker negligibly affects downstream task performance on average. In our copy-paste tests, windowbased detection consistently identifies copied passages containing as few as 1,536 consecutive tokens. Knowledge-based Visual Question Answering (VQA) requires reasoning that extends beyond visible image content, demanding external commonsense and factual knowledge. While recent work leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) as knowledge sources, the question of how deeply such guidance should be integrated into vision language architecture remains underexplored. In this work, we generate structured reasoning guidance, visual focus concepts, distractor elements and external knowledge using Llama 3.3 70B and systematically compare two integration strategies within unified frozen ViT + FLAN-T5 framework on the OK-VQA benchmark: decision level late fusion and attention-matrix injection. Contrary to the prevailing assumption that deeper integration is superior, we find that shallow late fusion achieves the strongest results (39.4% exact match, +10.0 over the no guidance baseline), outperforming deep attention modification (31.8%) under data scarce fine-tuning. We further contribute an LLM based augmentation pipeline that expands training data more than tenfold via question paraphrasing. Our findings indicate that integration depth must be matched to the data regime. Keywords: visual question answering, large language models, vision transformer, knowledge integration, multimodal fusion Visual Question Answering (VQA) is fundamental multimodal task requiring joint reasoning over an image and natural-language question to produce correct answer. Abstract Human videos provide rich motion targets for humanoid learning, yet visually plausible references can still produce persistent failures under physics-based execution. These failures reveal where training supervision should change. We present MimicX, policy-in-the-loop framework that uses execution feedback to refine video-driven humanoid motion tracking. Starting from reconstructed and retargeted motion, MimicX localizes difficult transitions and affected body regions, then jointly adapts tracking objectives and the reset curriculum for policy continuation. Task-averaged results show 25.7% reduction in body-tracking error and 255.6% increase in execution horizon. Additional video, motion-reference, and collision-scene studies evaluate the method beyond the core tasks, while MimicX-HLoop accelerates feedback through heterogeneous execution. Overall, MimicX turns policy failure into actionable supervision for deciding what to refine and which refinement to retain. Text:"G3VLA: Geometric Inductive Bias for Vision-Language-Action Models Yue Peng1, Yongzhe Zhao1, Artur Habuda2, Khuyen Pham3, Yanheng Zhu1, Tran Nguyen Le,2, Fares Abu-Dakka,4, and Li Guo,1 Abstract Vision-language-action (VLA) models have rapidly advanced generalist robot manipulation by harnessing the semantic knowledge of pretrained vision-language backbones. However, their visual representations are typically grounded in 2D image coordinates rather than calibrated camera geometry. This limitation is especially pronounced in multi-camera systems, where views are coupled by known intrinsics and extrinsics but processed as independent images. We propose G3VLA, camera-aware geometric module that injects calibrated structure into the visual-token stream of pretrained VLA without altering its action space or original actionlearning objective. G3VLA combines intrinsic-conditioned ray embeddings, projective positional encoding (PRoPE), and bidirectional cross-view fusion to establish geometrically consistent representations across camera views. Geometric supervision is provided by ground-truth point maps or confidence-gated π 3X teacher predictions, enabling training without depth sensors or manual geometric annotations. On π0, G3VLA consistently improves performance on LIBERO and RoboCasa24 under both supervision regimes, while ground-truth supervision improves five-task RoboTwin2.0 averages with three cameras. Experiments on camera-shifted LIBERO-Goal and real robots demonstrate improved robustness to unseen camera viewpoints. Controlled calibration studies across π0 and π0.5 show that these gains depend on correct camera geometry while remaining stable under mild calibration perturbations. These results demonstrate that grounding VLA visual tokens in calibrated camera geometry provides simple and general mechanism for improving geometric consistency, multi-view reasoning, and viewpoint robustness in pretrained robot policies. Text:"Walter Simoncini1, Dheeraj Varghese1, Gertjan J. Burghouts2, Cees G.M. Snoek1 1University of Amsterdam 2TNO, Intelligent Imaging Abstract. Scaling up transformers has produced models with broad capabilities, deployed across heterogeneous hardware and fluctuating workloads. Yet, model families offer only few fixed sizes (e.g., S, B, L), forcing users to commit to model based on hardware and latency constraints. This rigidity is often suboptimal under varying query loads. We introduce retraining-free method that elastifies pre-trained transformers into single model that adapts on-the-fly across continuum of computational budgets. Our key contribution is differentiable relaxation of the functional unit-ranking problem: unlike prior work, which optimizes single importance weight per MLP block or attention head, we rank every individual MLP neuron and attention head jointly, on common scale, by minimizing the models loss under soft pruning across many sparsity targets, all at once. Across 27 vision and text transformer encoders ranging from 22M to 8B parameters, our method yields smooth degradation up to 60% sparsity and outperforms one-shot baselines under deep pruning. For example, pruning DINOv3 ViT-H+/16 to 50% sparsity removes 427M parameters while reducing ImageNet-1k linear-probing accuracy by only 0.8%, without the need for weight correction or retraining. At 60% sparsity, pruned AugReg ViT-B/16 model achieves up to 1.80 higher throughput and 1.26 lower single-query latency on an A100 GPU. Elastic models are produced in minutes, with construction time scaling sub-linearly with parameter count. Correspondence: w.simoncini@uva.nl (cid:128) Website @ GitHub Figure 1 Differentiable soft pruning. Initial importance scores for MLP neurons and attention heads are transformed into learnable log-scores and median-centered by type, aligning their reference points without changing within-type rankings. At each optimization step, the joint ranking determines threshold κb and"

Text:"AgentSafeEvo: Adversarial Co-Evolution for LLM Agent Safety Chenxu Wang1,2, Xiaoyu Wen3,1, Han Qi1, Ziyuan Zhou1, Cheng Jin4, Chaochao Lu1, Qiaosheng Zhang1, 1Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory, 2Fudan University, 3Shanghai Jiaotong University, 4Zhejiang University wangchenxu@pjlab.org.cn, zhaoqiaosheng@pjlab.org.cn https://github.com/lalalamdbf/AgentSafeEvo https://huggingface.co/collections/lalalamdbf/agentsafeevo Large language model (LLM) agents accomplish complex tasks through multi-turn interactions with external tools. However, malicious content embedded in user queries, tool descriptions, or environment observations can manipulate their decisions and induce unsafe actions. Adversarial co-evolution has emerged as an effective approach to strengthening LLM safety, but existing work primarily focuses on language-level safety or indirect prompt injection in web tasks. In this paper, we introduce AgentSafeEvo, co-evolutionary reinforcement learning framework for agent safety built on an attackerdefender game spanning diverse tasks and attack types. The attacker learns to construct adversarial agent tasks that expose weaknesses in the defenders tool use, while the defender learns to complete tasks safely under these attacks. We further introduce learnability-guided task weighting, which uses within-task defender-reward variability to adjust each tasks contribution to attacker and defender updates. Extensive experiments on agent safety and general capability benchmarks demonstrate that our method improves adversarial robustness while maintaining general agent capabilities. Disclaimer: This paper contains potentially offensive and harmful text. 1. Introduction The emergence of large language model (LLM) agents marks shift from text generation to active interaction with external environments. Through multi-turn interactions with external tools, agents can search for information, process documents, and manage schedules to complete complex, multistep tasks [1,"

Abstract ATAS improves long-form video consistency by compiling screenplays into referenced shot requests, retrieving spatial and temporal evidence from scene and event memories, and conditionally updating memory after generation.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Long-form agentic video generation must turn a screenplay into shots that remain spatially and temporally consistent across nonconsecutive requests. Passing prompts or recent frames between shots does not provide a shared spatial frame for revisiting a scene or a time-indexed record for replaying an earlier event. We present Any Time, Any Space (ATAS), which compiles a screenplay into shot requests with explicit location, camera, and event-time references. Before generation, it retrieves relevant evidence from a scene memory of registered static geometry and an event memory of dynamic observations indexed by scene, event, and time. After generation, ATAS reconstructs and registers the clip. It updates memory only when review and state checks accept the observation, while film delivery remains independent of memory admission. Across four video generators, ATAS improves all reported cross-shot consistency measures and achieves the highest aggregate benchmark score for each backbone. It also outperforms the compared agents and 3D-guided methods on target-image spatial revisit, leads three event-recapture criteria, and receives the highest human ratings on all six criteria. The video results and code are available on our project page: https://lhmd.top/atas/.

Abstract Road MOPD reduces gradient conflicts in multi-teacher distillation by using cross-teacher agreement for token-level credit assignment and auxiliary gradients to constrain conflicting updates.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) extends on-policy distillation (OPD) by routing domain-specific data to specialized teachers, integrating their capabilities through dense token-level supervision on student-generated trajectories. However, domain routing only distinguishes the supervision sources for different domains; updates from all domains still act on shared parameters. When teachers differ substantially in response style and domain expertise, supervision that is effective in isolation may still lead to persistent gradient conflicts and capability degradation during joint optimization. To address this challenge, we propose Cross-Teacher Guarding for MOPD (Road MOPD), which introduces non-routed teachers as auxiliary cross-domain critics on the same student trajectories and coordinates multi-teacher supervision through token-level credit assignment and gradient updates. Specifically, cross-teacher-guided credit assignment uses teacher agreement to strengthen token-level corrections jointly supported by the teachers and attenuate conflicting signals; preventive multi-teacher gradient projection uses auxiliary gradients from the same trajectories to constrain potentially conflicting updates and adaptively controls projection strength to retain target-domain learning signals. In experiments, Road MOPD mitigates degradation in the disadvantaged domain, gains over 5 percentage points under high conflict, and improves overall performance in low-conflict and three-domain settings.

Abstract Player-1 improves game control by combining supervised fine-tuning on gameplay trajectories with reinforcement learning across synthetic environments, achieving substantial gains over demonstration-only baselines.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Computer-use agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in interpreting screens and executing keyboard and mouse actions. However, games, with their visually rich worlds and diverse mechanics, remain challenging and offer a demanding next step toward general digital control. Current game-agent learning pipelines often rely on large-scale demonstrations with limited coverage of dynamic game states, while scalable environments for interactive training remain difficult to obtain. We introduce Player-1, a post-training framework for learning game control beyond demonstrations through synthetic experience. We extend the computer-use action space with relative pointer motion, sustained inputs, and composite actions. For supervised initialization, we fine-tune computer-use models on 6,044 curated gameplay trajectories that pair recorded actions with task instructions and visual reasoning. Player-1-9B reaches 38.33% success compared with 12.63% for the strongest evaluated baseline, with further gains over SFT on ViZDoom-10 and GameWorld. Further analyses show that broader synthetic environment coverage improves transfer, while the use of composite actions that combine multiple keyboard and mouse inputs increases during RL. We demonstrate that learning from synthetic experience substantially improves game control beyond supervised initialization, with gains that generalize beyond the RL training suite.

Abstract Doc2Harness is a deterministic two-stage benchmark that evaluates long-horizon harness engineering by having models build and run harnesses across isolated containers, revealing substantial performance gaps among frontier models.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Agents driven by large language models are increasingly deployed on long-horizon tasks, where they use tools and modify real workspaces. How well they perform depends as much on the harness around the model as on the model itself. Existing benchmarks measure downstream task success under a fixed harness, leaving harness engineering itself unexplored. We propose Doc2Harness, a two-stage benchmark that evaluates long-horizon harness engineering directly. In Stage 1, a build model operating inside a fixed builder harness receives an interface-only contract skeleton and a development document that consolidates behavior across twelve frontier harnesses, and then constructs a complete candidate harness in an isolated container. In Stage 2, the frozen candidate harness runs in containers separate from the construction environment, one per test case, and is driven end to end by a scripted mock model. We score each test case using four observable channels, namely the requests sent to the model, the files on disk, the process exit code, and the command output; positive controls rule out vacuous passes. Across nineteen frontier models, each evaluated with three independent builds on 328 test cases, GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest pass rate at 53.05%, while the weakest model reaches 27.44%, a gap of roughly twenty-six percentage points. Even the strongest model fails nearly half of the test cases, and failures cluster around how cross-cutting functionality is split across modules. To our knowledge, Doc2Harness provides the first deterministic benchmark for measuring long-horizon harness engineering.

Abstract Researchers benchmarked ten self-distillation methods against GRPO across multiple agent tasks and model sizes, finding limited consistent gains, hidden training instability, and no reliable cost-to-performance benefit.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Self-distillation in agentic reinforcement learning has produced a growing number of methods. However, existing evaluations often reuse published baseline scores under differing experimental conditions, provide few direct comparisons among contemporaneous methods, and give limited attention to training stability and computational cost. These limitations obscure the source of reported gains and the practical strengths and weaknesses of competing methods. Costly agent training further motivates a common basis for comparison. To address these gaps, we compare ten representative methods against GRPO on ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, Search-QA, and WebShop across three model sizes. Our study comprises 132 training runs totaling 12,124.4 recorded H800 GPU-hours, with repeated evaluations over eight rollout seeds for the pretrained backbones and all eleven trained methods. Beyond final task performance, we examine training progress, stability, and measured computational cost. Our evaluation yields three key findings. First, gains over GRPO remain limited in scope, with no method consistently outperforming it across settings. Second, strong final performance can mask unstable training. Third, additional training cost does not consistently translate into better performance. We establish this benchmark to support fair, comprehensive comparison and guide future research toward reliable and cost-effective improvements in agent training. Project page: https://aim-uofa.github.io/Self-Distillation-AgentRL/

Abstract CriticOPD improves on-policy distillation by using teacher critiques to restore recoverability, enabling students to revise incorrect reasoning and achieve stronger mathematical reasoning performance.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student model on its own rollouts with token-level supervision from a teacher, assuming that the teacher can reach a correct answer from any prefix the student writes. We call this property the recoverability of the teacher and show it fails on incorrect rollouts: the teacher follows student errors, although it recognizes them. We propose CriticOPD, using error recognition to restore recoverability. The teacher critiques an incorrect rollout, allowing the student to rewrite its solution following the error. The resulting output, including the critique as privileged information, is scored by the teacher while the student uses the revision without the critique for training. On two Qwen3 students and six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, CriticOPD improves OPD, with the largest gains on competition problems.

Abstract Cortex consolidates context-induced improvements into mergeable low-rank or structural updates, preserving non-target capabilities across sequential edits and diverse model scales.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Context can improve large language model performance, but these gains may be lost after context removal. Inspired by biological systems consolidation, we formulate Memory Consolidation (MC) as internalizing these gains while preserving non-target capabilities, connecting context-specified editing, skill learning, and behavioral steering under a common objective. We introduce a five-task-family testbed measuring consolidation and retention under single, batch, and sequential updates. We propose Cortex, which generates prospective queries to anticipate uses of context and distills context-conditioned outputs into mergeable low-rank or structural feed-forward network updates. Across 4B--120B dense and mixture-of-experts backbones, including both text-only and multimodal models, Cortex lies on the empirical consolidation--retention Pareto frontier in most evaluated settings. After 50 sequential factual edits on a 14B model, Cortex-Structural recovers 88.9% of the context-induced gain while retaining 99.8% of original non-target performance.

Abstract LatentLoop improves looped Mixture-of-Experts by compressing the first loop visit, reallocating saved computation to larger hidden dimensions, and using separate attention parameters, yielding lower validation loss and better benchmark scores.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Looped Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models combine parameter reuse with sparse computation to improve performance under matched budgets. Prior work demonstrates that different loop visits can play different roles even when they share parameters. To probe the contributions of different loop visits, we learn separate residual coefficients in a model that applies a shared block twice, observing a smaller scale in the first visit. Fixed coefficient experiments show that reducing the first visit's scale preserves or improves performance, whereas the same reductions to the second hurt it. This asymmetry motivates us to explore asymmetric compute allocation. We propose LatentLoop, which compresses the first visit's sequence, retains full resolution in the second, and reallocates saved computation to increase the hidden size. We further learn how strongly the first visit updates the full sequence, and share MoE weights across visits while using separate attention parameters to accommodate their different sequence resolutions. Across 15 configurations spanning 1.3B--7B parameters, with the largest budget exceeding 10^{21} FLOPs, LatentLoop consistently lowers validation NLL. At a fitted NLL of 1.988, predicted compute savings are 19.0\% and 7.9\% relative to vanilla MoE and the looped baseline. It also improves average scores on nine benchmarks at all sizes in 0-shot and 5-shot settings. These results suggest asymmetric compute allocation is a promising direction for looped MoE models.

Abstract ActionOPD aligns smaller language-model agents with teachers via semantic action-level on-policy distillation, improving out-of-distribution tool-use performance without requiring shared tokenizers or teacher probabilities.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Distilling capable language-model agents into smaller models requires aligning the student's behavior with the teacher's. This raises a fundamental question: at which states and at what granularity should this alignment be performed? Offline imitation provides dense but token-specific supervision and can suffer from state-distribution mismatch. Token-level on-policy distillation (OPD) addresses this mismatch but typically requires teacher token probabilities and compatible tokenization. We argue that semantic actions provide a natural unit of supervision for agent distillation. We introduce ActionOPD, an action-level on-policy distillation method motivated by maximizing expected teacher-action likelihood at student-encountered states. At each such state, ActionOPD samples actions from both the student and a black-box teacher, rewarding student actions according to their semantic agreement with the sampled teacher actions. Through policy optimization, ActionOPD aligns the student's action-level behavior with the teacher's decisions without requiring reproduction of teacher-written reasoning or access to teacher token probabilities. Training Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B with two teachers on diverse, curated tool-use tasks yields average gains of 3.3-6.9 percentage points across four out-of-distribution benchmarks. ActionOPD scales favorably with student size and teacher capability, without requiring the teacher and student to share a model family or tokenizer. On τ^2-bench, ActionOPD achieves competitive performance against offline distillation and token-level OPD. We further explore Self-ActionOPD with a same-model teacher and privileged context. These results establish ActionOPD as a promising approach to agent distillation.

Abstract This work proposes a two-axis framework for multimodal agent memory covering information formation and adaptive management to support lifelong learning.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Multimodal models and agents have emerged to enable AI systems to learn from and interact with the real world more like humans by integrating information across multiple modalities. However, enabling multimodal agents to continually acquire useful information from complex, heterogeneous multimodal data remains a significant challenge as they move toward long-horizon tasks and lifelong settings. Addressing this challenge requires effective memory mechanisms that can maintain continuity across sessions, adapt to changing users and environments, and refine memory-management policies through accumulated experience. We introduce a two-axis analytical framework for multimodal agent memory structured around two key questions: how multimodal information is formed and represented (Axis I, Formation and Representation), and how its management adapts with accumulated experience (Axis II, Memory Management). Axis I, Formation and Representation, characterizes what information is retained and how it is encoded (e.g., modality-specific embeddings, textual and structured records, latent and parametric representations) to support subsequent reasoning and action. Axis II, Memory Management, distinguishes three tiers: T1 Static Memory reuses fixed memory; T2 Dynamic Memory updates memory through a stable policy; and T3 Self-Evolving Memory updates memory through an evolving policy. Based on this framework, we synthesize recurring design patterns and derive principles for balancing source fidelity with abstraction, maintaining temporal validity and provenance, grounding memory use in recoverable evidence, and evolving memory policies over time. Furthermore, we discuss the challenges and potential future directions for the memory mechanism of multimodal agents.

Abstract ReMaRk improves reinforcement learning for recommendation by scoring generated lists against all valid replies jointly, reducing training cost while matching or exceeding prior performance.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small A recommendation request on Reddit draws several replies, each naming different titles. When an LLM recommender is trained on such threads with reinforcement learning (RL), which replies should a generated list be rewarded against? The state-of-the-art recipe, Rank-GRPO, picks one reply per training example, so the advantage mostly records which reply was drawn (85% of its variance) rather than how good the list is. We argue that RL should train on the request, not on each answer, and propose ReMaRk: it scores every list once against the union of all replies' titles and samples each request equally. This makes RL cheaper. On seven backbones and three datasets, ReMaRk reaches the validation recall of 2,000 per-reply RL steps in 25 to 53% of the steps and leads at that budget; trained until neither improves, it is never significantly behind Rank-GRPO and in four of seven pairs reaches Rank-GRPO's best with fewer GPU-hours. The same reward helps on ProtoQA, where about 100 people answer each question, which points to a simple rule for RL with several valid human answers: reward them jointly.

Существующий подход Rank-GRPO выбирает один ответ на каждый пример, из-за чего advantage в основном отражает случайный выбор ответа, а не качество списка. Abstract LensErase is a mask-based erasure foundation model pretrained on a large paired dataset and refined with reinforcement learning to handle diverse real-world removal tasks while preserving surrounding content.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We introduce LensErase, a general-purpose mask-based Erase Anything foundation model designed to cover a broad spectrum of real-world erase scenarios. LensErase supports diverse objects, visual text, watermarks, associated visual effects, multiple-object removal, and a wide range of object scales, aspect ratios, and image resolutions. First, to enable such broad coverage, we construct LensErase-100M, a large-scale dataset containing 101.07 million paired source-mask-target examples spanning diverse erase scenarios. Second, we pretrain LensErase from the text-to-image model Lens, adapting its strong generative prior to mask-based erasing. To efficiently incorporate the source image and erase mask, we propose Mask-Aware RoPE, a 3D rotary positional encoding that directly injects mask information into the source-image tokens without introducing a separate mask token sequence. Finally, we refine the model through supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning with contract-guided, region-aware rewards, and apply TraceFusion at inference to preserve high-fidelity content outside the effective edit region. Across six public benchmarks and the proposed LensErase-Bench, LensErase achieves substantial improvements under both reference-based objective metrics and reference-free VLM evaluation. We hope LensErase can serve as a foundational model for erase-related applications and systems.

Abstract KV4-RL accelerates reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards via INT4 KV cache quantization with selective INT8 protection, achieving substantial speedups with minimal accuracy loss.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Asynchronous rollout improves the efficiency of reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) by reducing under-utilization during long-tail generation. Sustained concurrency also concentrates context processing into fewer decode steps, increasing the KV workload per step. We characterize this KV concentration and find that KV cache quantization delivers substantially greater acceleration than weight-only and weight-activation quantization in our matched-workload comparisons. However, INT4 KV rollout can degrade learning even with token-level truncated importance sampling (TIS). We propose KV4-RL, which combines calibration-free INT4 KV quantization with selective INT8 protection of attention sinks and recent context. Protecting a bounded number of positions reduces sampler mismatch while retaining most of the speedup from INT4. Across 400 iterations of Qwen3-8B-Base GRPO training, KV4-RL achieves 1.80times cumulative rollout speedup and 1.27times cumulative full-iteration speedup over BF16 rollout, reaching 1.97times rollout speedup over the final 50 iterations. Under a common BF16 evaluator, its final six-benchmark mean accuracy is within 0.5 percentage points of BF16-rollout training for both Qwen3-4B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base.

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract KV4-RL accelerates reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards via INT4 KV cache quantization with selective INT8 protection, achieving substantial speedups with minimal accuracy loss.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Asynchronous rollout improves the efficiency of reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) by reducing under-utilization during long-tail generation. Sustained concurrency also concentrates context processing into fewer decode steps, increasing the KV workload per step. We characterize this KV concentration and find that KV cache quantization delivers substantially greater acceleration than weight-only and weight-activation quantization in our matched-workload comparisons. However, INT4 KV rollout can degrade learning even with token-level truncated importance sampling (TIS). We propose KV4-RL, which combines calibration-free INT4 KV quantization with selective INT8 protection of attention sinks and recent context. Protecting a bounded number of positions reduces sampler mismatch while retaining most of the speedup from INT4. Across 400 iterations of Qwen3-8B-Base GRPO training, KV4-RL achieves 1.80times cumulative rollout speedup and 1.27times cumulative full-iteration speedup over BF16 rollout, reaching 1.97times rollout speedup over the final 50 iterations. Under a common BF16 evaluator, its final six-benchmark mean accuracy is within 0.5 percentage points of BF16-rollout training for both Qwen3-4B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base."

Abstract This survey unifies research on agentic robots by tracing the evolution from robot policies to integrated systems using vision-language-action models, world models, and structured functional domains.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Foundation models are driving a paradigm shift in robotic intelligence, moving beyond low-level action prediction to goal-directed orchestration of data collection, model training, task planning, and real-world execution. We characterize this emerging paradigm as an agentic robots, in which agents reason over human instructions, interact with robotic environments, rectify execution errors autonomously, and dynamically adjust operational strategies. Although this field develops fast, current studies are not well connected. Researchers have no consistent view of how to combine different agentic functions into a complete robot system. This paper provides a unified overview of existing research on agentic robots. We first trace the development from robot policies to agentic systems, covering vision-language-action models, world models, and world-action models. We then clarify the definition and scope of agentic robots and organize existing research into seventeen categories across four functional domains: Model, Data, Environment, and Harness. Across these domains, we examine how agents shape model learning and robot decisions, collect and curate robot data, construct worlds, tasks, and tests for learning and evaluation, and organize capabilities for robot use. Finally, we discuss open challenges and outline directions for future research. Looking forward, we hope this work contributes to a clearer understanding of agentic robots and supports new approaches to making them more capable, reliable, and adaptive.

Abstract Hybrid Reasoning Agents combine latent internal reasoning with text-based external interaction via a unified architecture and specialized post-training, improving agent performance while reducing inference cost and token usage.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small How can we build an agent that thinks beyond human language, yet still acts through it in the external environment? Modern LLM agents still reason and interact through text, while latent reasoning offers an efficient internal medium but lacks native tool access and reasoning transparency. We bridge these two directions with Hybrid Reasoning Agents (HybridAgents), which dynamically navigate between latent reasoning and text-based interaction throughout a unified generation process. Architecturally, a lightweight HybridLink enables seamless transitions between text and latent modes, while the agent's continuous working memory preserves lossless hybrid context across multiple turns. These designs preserve tool-integrated agentic capabilities while making latent thoughts directly inspectable. Building on this architecture, we further develop dedicated post-training algorithms for hybrid reasoning: HybridSFT stabilizes tool-integrated hybrid generation by teaching the backbone to coordinate with HybridLink, then HybridRL scales reasoning by learning when to think in latent space and when to act through text. We evaluate HybridAgent at 3 model scales across 11 benchmarks covering mathematics, science, medicine, search, and agentic reasoning. Compared with frontier LLM Agents and agentic frameworks spanning both text-based and latent methods, HybridAgent consistently improves performance by 10.4%, while delivering 35.5%-54.7% token reduction, 1.8x-2.6x faster inference, and 2.7x-3.7x cost savings. Code and data are available at https://github.com/togethercomputer/HybridAgent.

Лёгкий модуль HybridLink обеспечивает плавные переходы между режимами, а непрерывная рабочая память сохраняет контекст без потерь на протяжении нескольких шагов диалога. Abstract RuleTrek is a multi-domain benchmark that evaluates large language model agents on identifying, deducing, and applying domain-specific rules across complex, multi-file scenarios, revealing substantial performance gaps.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language models (LLMs) increasingly tackle complex real-world tasks in specialized domains. However, identifying and correctly applying domain-specific rules remains challenging when scenario information is distributed across multiple files. Existing rule-reasoning benchmarks typically represent scenarios using only a few factual statements, simplifying the identification and application of relevant rules. To address this gap, this paper introduces RuleTrek, a multi-domain benchmark for agentic rule reasoning in complex scenarios. Its rule-grounded construction pipeline extracts structured rule graphs, generates and incrementally composes rule-specific sub-scenarios while ensuring consistency and solvability. To systematically evaluate agentic rule reasoning in complex scenarios, RuleTrek assesses three capabilities in interactive workspaces: Rule Identification (RI), Rule Deduction (RD), and End-to-End Rule Reasoning (E2E). Experiments on 11 frontier LLMs show that the highest RI and RD scores are only 62.47\% and 60.43\%, respectively, while E2E scores range from 26.97\% to 55.29\%. These results demonstrate that current LLM agents face substantial challenges in rule reasoning over complex scenarios. We make our code and dataset publicly available at https://github.com/yuan247/RuleTrek{RuleTrek}.

Abstract Real-world super-resolution is improved by mapping degraded image embeddings back to a high-resolution manifold within a frozen DINOv3-L latent space, yielding high fidelity and fast inference.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small In an image latent space, the embeddings of high-resolution, natural, and sharp images form a manifold. Degradation of high-resolution images pushes their embeddings off this manifold. Real-world super-resolution (SR) then becomes the task of mapping the degraded embedding back onto this manifold --- not anywhere on the manifold, but to the point that preserves what the input still carries, both its semantics and pixel details. Every published method implements this mapping in a reconstruction-oriented latent space or pixel space. We claim these spaces are the wrong substrates for SR. Low-resolution and degraded images are embedded far from the manifold, making the mapping difficult and expensive. The lack of semantic information in these substrates also makes it difficult to navigate to the faithful point on the manifold, causing severe hallucination when degradation is heavy. Thus, restoring in a suitable latent space is crucial to the SR task. We show that the latent space of 23 fused layers of a frozen DINOv3-L is one such space that makes the SR task easier. Degraded images are embedded near the manifold. Moreover, this substrate contains a hierarchy of information, from pixel record to degradation robust semantics, guiding the model to find the faithful point on the manifold. On this substrate, a 415M decoder is trained under reconstruction and adversarial objectives to map the degraded embeddings back and decode to pixel space in one pass. The resulting model, RAESR, attains the best fidelity--perception trade-off among state-of-the-art adversarial and diffusion-based restorers on RealSR, DRealSR, LSDIR and DIV2K-Val, at 37 ms per 512 by 512 image on a single H20 GPU. Swapping the substrate for a VAE latent under an identical recipe loses on every metric.
[15.10.2026 15:36] Response: {"desc": "Статья предлагает новый подход к реальной супер-резолюции (Real-world SR): деградированные изображения переносятся на многообразие чётких изображений в латентном пространстве замороженного DINOv3-L, а не в пиксельном или VAE-пространстве. Авторы показывают, что эмбеддинги из 23 объединённых слоёв DINOv3 лежат близко к многообразию и содержат иерархию признаков от пикселей до устойчивой к деградациям семантики, что снижает галлюцинации. Декодер на 415M параметров обучается с функциями реконструкции и состязательными (adversarial) потерями и восстанавливает изображение за один проход. Модель RAESR достигает лучшего баланса между точностью и перцептивным качеством среди современных GAN- и diffusion-методов при скорости 37 мс на изображение 512x512.", "emoji": "🖼️", "title": "Восстанавливаем картинку там, где живёт смысл: в семантическом латентном пространстве"}

Abstract HumanoidToolBench evaluates humanoid robots on tool selection and coordinated manipulation and locomotion across diverse tasks, revealing gaps between selection and execution.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small As robotic hardware and learning methods advance, humanoids need tools to perform tasks beyond their inherent physical limits. Successful tool use requires selecting a suitable tool and coordinating manipulation and, when needed, locomotion to complete the task. Existing benchmarks do not jointly evaluate these capabilities on a humanoid. We introduce HumanoidToolBench, an 18-task benchmark spanning three scenarios, three execution levels, and two tool-set modes, together with ToolBook, a dataset of 3.1k demonstrations collected in simulation and on a real Unitree G1. Evaluation of seven policies in simulation and three on the real robot reveals substantial gaps between selecting a suitable tool and completing the task. Focused GR00T N1.7 probes show reduced selection accuracy on unseen tools and continued task execution under unrelated instructions. Code and data are available at https://snu-pi.github.io/HumanoidToolBench/.

"Abstract HumanoidToolBench evaluates humanoid robots on tool selection and coordinated manipulation and locomotion across diverse tasks, revealing gaps between selection and execution.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small As robotic hardware and learning methods advance, humanoids need tools to perform tasks beyond their inherent physical limits. Successful tool use requires selecting a suitable tool and coordinating manipulation and, when needed, locomotion to complete the task. Existing benchmarks do not jointly evaluate these capabilities on a humanoid. We introduce HumanoidToolBench, an 18-task benchmark spanning three scenarios, three execution levels, and two tool-set modes, together with ToolBook, a dataset of 3.1k demonstrations collected in simulation and on a real Unitree G1. Evaluation of seven policies in simulation and three on the real robot reveals substantial gaps between selecting a suitable tool and completing the task. Focused GR00T N1.7 probes show reduced selection accuracy on unseen tools and continued task execution under unrelated instructions. Code and data are available at https://snu-pi.github.io/HumanoidToolBench/."

Abstract Reinforcement learning gains in hybrid reasoning models' NO-THINK mode largely stem from reactivating existing THINK-mode reasoning rather than learning new capabilities.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Hybrid reasoning models such as the Qwen3 series switch between a THINK mode and a NO-THINK mode through a few chat-template tokens. A popular recipe trains such models with reinforcement learning in NO-THINK mode and reports improved reasoning performance. We provide evidence that much of this gain can be explained by thinking leakage: the gain comes largely from partially reactivating the reasoning abilities of the original model's THINK mode instead of learning enhanced reasoning behavior. We train Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B with GRPO in NO-THINK mode. As accuracy improves, responses become longer and more reflective, are increasingly judged by the original model to resemble its own thinking, and the model's internal activations shift toward its THINK mode. We then investigate at the level of weights, activations, and attention heads: scaling the RL update further elicits THINK-mode behavior and approaches THINK-mode accuracy but does not exceed it; in Qwen3-8B, replacing a low-dimensional THINK-related activation subspace of the RL-trained model with its original-model value removes most of the RL gain, and the reverse exchange transfers a considerable fraction of the gain to the original model; and the RL-induced shift passes through the attention heads that read the empty think block. We further identify a possible mechanism in the GRPO training signal: responses favored by the advantage-weighted update are more THINK-like, and a gradient step computed from this signal moves the model toward its THINK state. Thus, gains in NO-THINK mode can largely reflect reactivation of reasoning abilities already present in the original model.

Abstract APT is an automated framework that adaptively post-trains text-to-image models by using agents to evaluate capabilities, diagnose failures, and generate targeted reinforcement learning data in a self-improving loop.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Post-training is crucial for improving text-to-image models, yet existing pipelines typically rely on manually curated training data and fixed training distributions. We introduce APT (Agentic Post-Training), a fully automated framework that adaptively post-trains a base model according to its evolving capability profile. APT coordinates three agents to construct evaluation sets from user requirements, diagnose model-specific strengths and failure modes, and generate reinforcement learning data with an adaptive prompt distribution that emphasizes underperforming capabilities. By repeatedly evaluating, diagnosing, and updating the generator, APT lets the current model's behavior shape its next training tasks, forming a feedback loop inspired by recursive self-improvement. For general-purpose settings without user-specified evaluation scenarios, we further develop an evaluation harness that enables broad and diverse evaluation set generation. Experiments across multiple base models, benchmarks, and generation architectures demonstrate that APT is model-agnostic, benchmark-agnostic, architecture-agnostic, and fully automated.

Abstract SYNC enables heterogeneous language models to share selective object facts via word indices and receiver-local hidden-state directions without fine-tuning or translators.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Multi-agent systems allow language models to solve tasks collaboratively by sharing complementary information. These systems typically communicate through text, while recent approaches also explore exchanging internal model states. However, sharing these states across architectures often requires learned translators or assumptions about model compatibility. We introduce SYNC (Symbol-Indexed Neural Communication), a word-indexed interface that connects heterogeneous models without fine-tuning or a learned translator. From descriptions only it can see, the sender selects words from a shared list and sends their indices for each object, without seeing the questions. The receiver writes the described facts into its hidden states using its own word directions, vectors fitted once from its activations. We test selective use by swapping one attribute between two objects and requiring correct answers about both changed and unchanged attributes before and after the swap, four answers in total. We choose an early write layer on four development receivers and then fix it. With three different senders and nine newer receivers from five families, four-answer success on new scenes reaches 72.0-98.3%, up to 96 percentage points above middle-layer writing, and the two largest receivers match plain text. A task-free probe predicted in advance which depths each new receiver can use (45 of 45 cases). Without retuning, messages listing every fact keep 89.6-100% success under new layouts, and sender messages reach 23.9-64.0% on a landmark task with multi-token symbols, below text but from 0% without a message. These results show that shared word indices and receiver-local directions can support selective fact use across model families without additional training.

Abstract LabInstruct evaluates AI-generated instructional videos for real laboratory tasks and reveals that visual plausibility does not ensure procedural correctness.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) aim to automate the full experimental loop, from scientific decision-making to physical execution. Ideally, AI-generated plans could be carried out directly by robotic systems, but reliable automation remains difficult in complex, open-world laboratory environments, where experiments often involve fine-grained manipulation, long-horizon procedures, and substantial variation across tasks and setups. Humans therefore remain an important execution interface between AI-generated plans and physical experiments, creating a need for clear and effective human-facing experimental guidance. Because laboratory procedures are inherently visual, spatial, and dynamic, video is particularly well suited to communicating apparatus configurations, manipulation actions, temporal dependencies, and state changes. Recent advances in video generation now make it possible to synthesize experimental demonstrations directly from an initial workspace image and a natural-language instruction. However, whether such models can reliably communicate real laboratory procedures has not been systematically studied. We introduce LabInstruct, a benchmark for situated instructional video generation in real laboratories. LabInstruct contains 204 tasks across 5 scientific disciplines, with real reference executions and structured annotations of objects, actions, contacts, and state transitions. Evaluating 8 frontier image-to-video models, we find that visually plausible generations frequently remain procedurally incorrect, revealing a substantial gap between visual realism and the reliability required for experimental instruction.

Abstract SwarmRSI evolves scientific agent swarms through feedback-guided mutation and complementary recombination to enable recursive self-improvement across research tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Recursive self-improvement (RSI) in scientific research aims to improve agents' research capabilities through their own experimental experience. Scientific agent swarms can combine evidence and implementations from different research directions to guide their agents' subsequent experiments. However, poor experimental results can leave unclear whether to revise a hypothesis or its implementation, while overall program scores can hide components useful to other agents. To address these challenges, we introduce SwarmRSI, a framework that evolves scientific agent swarms for recursive self-improvement. We devise Feedback-Guided Swarm Mutation to refine hypotheses and their implementations using experimental feedback from other agents. We also propose Complementary Swarm Recombination to reuse retained hypotheses and complementary program components across agents, allowing partial results to support new research. Recursive Swarm Evolution integrates both mechanisms into a feedback loop, using new experimental evidence to guide subsequent research. Experiments on OpenProblems, NatureBench and MLE-Bench Lite demonstrate SwarmRSI's state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance. Further evaluations show that accumulated research experience improves performance on held-out tasks. Biologists also reproduce its programs for follow-up biological analyses.

Abstract A benchmark and data pipeline evaluate and improve LLM tool-calling agents under emotionally varied user interactions by filtering ineffective trajectories and scaling supervised training.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small In long-horizon tool-calling tasks, LLM agents can fail not only because of tool-use errors, but also because of ineffective interaction with users. Yet existing benchmarks largely assume cooperative and behaviorally stable users, leaving the effect of emotional variation on agent reliability underexplored. We introduce τ-Emotion-Bench, a ten-domain benchmark for evaluating tool-calling agents under controlled emotion-conditioned user interactions while preserving the underlying task objective. To scale both benchmark construction and agent training, we introduce Tracer, a scalable agentic data-generation pipeline that constructs grounded, policy-valid tasks and collects trajectories through an interactive agent--user--environment harness. By allowing dissatisfied users to terminate inefficient interactions, the harness provides an interaction-aware signal that filters trajectories with excessive user dependence and favors effective tool use. Experiments with two user simulators show that frontier agents remain unreliable, with substantial gaps between average task success and consistent task completion. Tracer-generated supervision consistently improves Qwen3 models across scales, generalizes to unseen domains, and transfers to τ-Bench and τ-Trait. Behavioral analyses further show that higher task success is associated with a better balance between tool execution and necessary user follow-up. Overall, τ-Emotion-Bench and Tracer provide a scalable framework for evaluating agent reliability under diverse user behaviors and improving tool-calling agents through interaction-aware supervision.

Abstract Rate-Adaptive Rollout enables interactive world models to generate video at variable temporal rates with consistent dynamics and extended physical horizons.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Video-based interactive world models aim to enable real-time, user-controllable environments. Yet most existing systems are trained and rolled out at a fixed temporal rate, limiting both runtime-rate adaptation and physical-horizon coverage -- a limitation we term temporal-rate coupling. Specifically, when runtime generation throughput differs from the learned temporal rate, the generated world evolves unintentionally more slowly or quickly. Moreover, using a single temporal rate imposes an inherent trade-off between temporal granularity and physical horizon: higher rates capture finer motion but cover a shorter duration under a fixed context window. To address both limitations, we introduce Rate-Adaptive Rollout (RAR), a framework that enables interactive world models to roll out at controllable temporal rates. At its core, Stride-RoPE aligns latent frames sampled at different rates on a shared physical-time coordinate, enabling consistent multi-rate rollout, unseen-rate generalization, and online rate switching without discarding history. Meanwhile, RAR uses multi-stride video sampling to enable joint learning of fine-grained dynamics and long-horizon evolution. Across action- and camera-controlled environments, RAR achieves temporally consistent multi-rate rollout and online rate switching while preserving high-rate generation quality and extending physical horizons under matched training budgets. RAR also transfers to pretrained world models while preserving perceptual quality.

Abstract SkillMarker embeds watermarks into agent skills while preserving instruction accuracy to enable ownership verification and leakage tracing.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Agent skills encode operational procedures and domain expertise as reusable knowledge assets, yet their distribution in plaintext exposes them to unauthorized copying and leakage. However, practical mechanisms for skill ownership verification and leakage tracing remain underdeveloped. Watermark embedding before distribution offers a potential solution, but structured, compact skill instructions impose stringent requirements for factual and procedural accuracy. To address these challenges, we introduce SkillMarker, a KGW-based watermarking method that jointly targets functionality preservation and strong watermark embedding. SkillMarker unifies quality-constrained rewriting with signal-oriented candidate search. The former separates essential knowledge from flexible expression to preserve instruction fidelity; the latter explores alternative expressions to obtain a stronger watermark signal under preservation constraints. We release an open-source toolkit that integrates watermark embedding, quality auditing, and detection to support the protection of agent skills. SkillMarker targets ownership verification and leakage tracing in distributed skill ecosystems, where content may be copied in whole or in part, or incorporated into other skills.

"Abstract SkillMarker embeds watermarks into agent skills while preserving instruction accuracy to enable ownership verification and leakage tracing.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Agent skills encode operational procedures and domain expertise as reusable knowledge assets, yet their distribution in plaintext exposes them to unauthorized copying and leakage. However, practical mechanisms for skill ownership verification and leakage tracing remain underdeveloped. Watermark embedding before distribution offers a potential solution, but structured, compact skill instructions impose stringent requirements for factual and procedural accuracy. To address these challenges, we introduce SkillMarker, a KGW-based watermarking method that jointly targets functionality preservation and strong watermark embedding. SkillMarker unifies quality-constrained rewriting with signal-oriented candidate search. The former separates essential knowledge from flexible expression to preserve instruction fidelity; the latter explores alternative expressions to obtain a stronger watermark signal under preservation constraints. We release an open-source toolkit that integrates watermark embedding, quality auditing, and detection to support the protection of agent skills. SkillMarker targets ownership verification and leakage tracing in distributed skill ecosystems, where content may be copied in whole or in part, or incorporated into other skills."

Abstract ScreenRef provides multimodal GUI grounding annotations across desktop, web, and mobile environments, and the D2RL training approach improves model generalization to achieve state-of-the-art results among 8B-scale agents.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Constructing robust GUI agents relies on multimodal visual grounding: the ability to precisely map natural language intents to actionable on-screen components. Despite abundant desktop, web, and mobile benchmarks, high-fidelity resources tailored for diverse environments are surprisingly scarce. To bridge this gap, we introduce the Screen Referring Grounding Dataset (ScreenRef), a comprehensive GUI grounding dataset consisting of GUI screenshots paired with natural language referring expressions and box or point annotations. Spanning desktop, web, and mobile domains, ScreenRef comprises 586K screenshots and over 633K referring expression instances, covering tasks such as text recognition, visual matching, spatial understanding, and refusal scenarios. To boost the model's grounding performance, we propose the Distillation-to-Reinforcement Learning (D2RL) method to address the limited generalization of supervised fine-tuning and the sparse reward problem in reinforcement learning. GroundGUI-8B achieves 95.2% on ScreenSpot-V2, 63.9% on ScreenSpot-Pro, 65.4% on OSWorld-G, 86.8% on MMBench-GUI L2, and 38.3% on UI-Vision. Among 8B-scale models, it produces substantial gains over models trained on open-source datasets, with performance comparable or even superior to models trained on closed-source data. Code, models, and datasets are available at https://github.com/PrentisAI/GroundGUI.

Abstract Apertus 1.5 extends open multimodal language models to image and audio reasoning with long-context training, reinforcement learning, and fully open data and weights.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Language models are becoming infrastructure, and infrastructure built in the open, by public institutions, on documented data, is the alternative to dependence on a few providers; Apertus shows this is viable at scale. Apertus 1.5 extends the fully open Apertus family from text to multimodal reasoning. Continued pretraining of the Apertus 1.0 models, on 4T additional tokens for the 8B and 2T for the 70B, adds image and audio understanding and extends the context window fourfold to 262,144 tokens; multimodality enters through the tokenizer, the language-model head remains restricted to text, and the architecture is unchanged. Supervised finetuning, long-context finetuning, reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, and preference alignment complete the released models. Thinking is a flag of the chat template rather than a separate model, and lifts mathematical reasoning by up to 20 points on the 8B model. The released models improve over their predecessors on nearly every benchmark; among fully open models of its class, the 8B leads on knowledge, instruction following, and cultural benchmarks, and its instruction following surpasses all compared models, including open-weight ones. On image and audio understanding, a first for the family, the models are competitive with fully open multimodal models, strongest on document understanding and long-form and lower-resource speech recognition, while specialized vision and audio models remain ahead on perception and transcription of English and other high-resource languages. As in Apertus 1.0, we train only on openly available data, respect crawler opt-outs retroactively, and exclude restrictively licensed material. We release the model weights, data preparation and training recipes, and evaluation suites under permissive licenses; openness extends to values, with the Apertus charter guiding alignment stages published alongside the models.

Abstract ProgAgent introduces a programmatic tool-calling training framework that improves long-horizon agent performance by using executable code and runtime variables instead of sequential context accumulation.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly expected to orchestrate diverse tools over long-horizon tasks. Existing agents typically use Direct Tool Calling (DTC), where the model invokes tools sequentially, and tool responses accumulate in the model context, which can lead to context rot and orchestration breakdown. In contrast, Programmatic Tool Calling (PTC) invokes tools through executable code and stores intermediate results in runtime variables rather than appending them to the context. Prior work has explored PTC mainly as an inference-time optimization, but its benefits for agentic training remain unclear, and suitable verifiable training tasks are scarce. In this work, we introduce ProgAgent, a full PTC training stack, comprising a pipeline for synthesizing verifiable long-horizon tasks, a lightweight PTC runtime, and a two-stage training recipe. Using only 215 tasks instantiated at four environment scales, ProgAgent yields higher pass rates than the evaluated baselines of comparable size across four benchmarks. On LOCA-bench, ProgAgent-14B achieves a 30.7% pass rate at a 64k-token environment state, where these baselines score zero. Cross-harness experiments show that PTC-trained models outperform DTC-trained models under both evaluation interfaces, suggesting that the benefits of PTC training extend beyond the interface.

Abstract PixText refines local text in generated images via grouped pixel-space generation with MeanFlow, improving glyph accuracy while preserving backgrounds and reducing inference cost.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Generated images often have coherent layouts and visual content, yet local errors in small text and complex characters still limit their readability and practical use. Scene-text refinement requires accuracy, preservation, and efficiency. Existing methods based on variational autoencoders (VAEs) can lose fine strokes during reconstruction, while full-image editing can shift background colors. In this work, we introduce PixText, a model that formulates local text refinement as grouped image generation in pixel space. Conditioned on the original image, PixText generates only the target regions, preserves unedited content, and uses MeanFlow for four-step generation. We further introduce Text Refinement Bench to evaluate text correction and image preservation in generated images. On this benchmark, PixText achieves 83.06% glyph pass and 94.66% exact target-character OCR accuracy, versus 36.77% and 52.31% for the strongest baseline. Its four-step MeanFlow variant retains an 81.84% pass rate with less than one GPU-second of amortized generation cost per image.
